- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores machine learning and language models for multimodal depression detection, specifically addressing the Multimodal Personality-Aware Depression Detection (MPDD) Challenge. It compares the performance of XGBoost, transformer-based architectures, and large language models (LLMs) on audio, video, and text features of the MPDD dataset. The authors analyze the strengths and weaknesses of each model type in capturing depression-related signals across different modalities to improve mental health prediction. The models are evaluated using weighted and unweighted F1 scores, comparing the performance of 1s and 5s window segments across multiple datasets, binary and ternary depression level classification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Comparison:** The paper provides a systematic comparison of three different modeling approaches (XGBoost, Transformers, and LLMs) within the same context. This direct comparison is valuable for understanding the relative strengths of each.
    *   **Multimodal Integration:** It addresses a key challenge in mental health analysis: effectively integrating information from different modalities (audio, visual, and text).
    *   **Practical Application:**  The study is grounded in a real-world depression detection challenge (MPDD), increasing its practical relevance.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insight into the impact of different components like class weighting, PCA, mixup augmentation, and cross-validation, providing a better understanding of how to improve individual models
    *   **Efficiency and Size Analysis**: Comparing models like Transformer and LLM to simpler models shows important findings on which model size performs best for the limited data size

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The models used are not novel themselves; rather, the novelty comes from the application of these methods to a new dataset (MPDD) and the comparison between them. This reduces the impact somewhat, as the methods' performance is not unexpected based on prior work.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The findings are largely specific to the MPDD dataset. While this is the focus of the challenge, generalizability to other datasets with different characteristics is unclear.
    *   **LLM Performance:** The LLM performance is particularly weak and may need extra improvement, such as prompting strategy or LoRA training. LLM usage and methodology could be improved to reflect higher level of fine-tuning.
    *   **Limited exploration of more advanced LLM methods:** While the paper is based on research from 2025, and therefore can be given some leeway as newer technology was not available, exploration of other LLM techniques that have been adopted in the past year could give the paper some added value.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the understanding of how different machine learning approaches perform on multimodal depression detection tasks. The comparison can help researchers choose the most appropriate method for specific datasets or applications.
    *   The analysis of feature importance and model behavior could inform the development of more effective and interpretable depression detection systems.
    *   The findings highlight the importance of dataset characteristics and the need for careful tuning and optimization of models for specific tasks.

**Overall:**

The paper has merit in providing a structured comparison of different models on a practical problem. However, the limited novelty of the methods and the LLM's performance limitations, combined with the dataset-specific nature of the results, reduce its impact. While the comprehensive analysis is valuable, the study could benefit from more in-depth exploration of innovative approaches or more effective LLM integration.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a competent and well-executed comparison of existing methods. The MPDD challenge context gives it practical relevance. However, the lack of truly novel methodological contributions, particularly in LLM usage, and the dataset-dependent nature of the results limit its overall impact. The ablation studies add a solid level of analysis. The paper provides some insight into which model is best for multimodal depression detection but does not necessarily push the field significantly forward. A score of 6 acknowledges the paper's value as a systematic comparison while reflecting its limitations in terms of originality and groundbreaking impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### cMALC-D: Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20818v1)
- **Authors**: Anirudh Satheesh, Keenan Powell, Hua Wei
- **Abstract**: Many multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms are trained in fixed simulation environments, making them brittle when deployed in real-world scenarios with more complex and uncertain conditions. Contextual MARL (cMARL) addresses this by parameterizing environments with context variables and training a context-agnostic policy that performs well across all environment configurations. Existing cMARL methods attempt to use curriculum learning to help train and evaluate context-agnostic policies, but they often rely on unreliable proxy signals, such as value estimates or generalized advantage estimates that are noisy and unstable in multi-agent settings due to inter-agent dynamics and partial observability. To address these issues, we propose Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending (cMALC-D), a framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate semantically meaningful curricula and provide a more robust evaluation signal. To prevent mode collapse and encourage exploration, we introduce a novel diversity-based context blending mechanism that creates new training scenarios by combining features from prior contexts. Experiments in traffic signal control domains demonstrate that cMALC-D significantly improves both generalization and sample efficiency compared to existing curriculum learning baselines. We provide code at https://github.com/DaRL-LibSignal/cMALC-D.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces cMALC-D, a novel framework for contextual multi-agent reinforcement learning (CMARL) that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to guide curriculum learning with a diversity-based context blending mechanism. cMALC-D addresses the limitations of existing CMARL methods, which often rely on noisy proxy signals for evaluation or handcrafted curricula, and can struggle to generalize to unseen environments. The framework uses an LLM as a high-level controller to generate semantically meaningful curricula based on the agent's learning progress, while the diversity-based context blending prevents mode collapse and encourages exploration. Experiments in traffic signal control domains demonstrate that cMALC-D significantly improves generalization and sample efficiency compared to existing curriculum learning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the integration of LLMs for curriculum generation within the CMARL setting and the introduction of a diversity-based context blending method. Using LLMs for curriculum design is a relatively new concept, although some prior work exists in single-agent settings. The diversity-based blending mechanism is a practical addition that addresses a common problem in curriculum learning. The paper effectively combines these two elements into a coherent framework.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in MARL: generalization to unseen environments. CMARL is an important area of research, and improving its ability to handle complex and uncertain conditions has significant real-world implications. The performance gains demonstrated in the traffic signal control experiments, a relevant and challenging application domain, suggest that cMALC-D has the potential to advance the state-of-the-art in CMARL. The LLM approach introduces a new paradigm for generating curriculums.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a key problem:** Generalization in MARL.
    *   **Novel approach:** Combining LLMs and diversity-based blending for curriculum generation.
    *   **Solid experimental results:** Demonstrates improved generalization and sample efficiency.
    *   **Well-defined framework:**  Clear explanation of the cMALC-D architecture and algorithm.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance is highly dependent on the capabilities of the LLM. The choice of LLM (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct) and its prompt design can significantly affect the results. A more extensive analysis of the LLM's impact and sensitivity would strengthen the paper. Further LLM sensitivity studies need to be performed.
    *   **Limited application domain:** The experiments are limited to traffic signal control. While this is a relevant application, it's important to evaluate cMALC-D in other MARL domains to assess its broader applicability.
    *   **Limited analysis of LLM context:** While the paper mentions that the LLM identifies correlations between the features, it doesn't go into depth with the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** cMALC-D has the potential to influence the development of more robust and generalizable MARL algorithms. The use of LLMs for curriculum generation could inspire new research directions in MARL and other areas of reinforcement learning. The practical context blending mechanism is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated framework for CMARL with strong experimental results. The limitations regarding the LLM dependency and limited application domains are acknowledged, but don't detract significantly from the contribution. The practical application to traffic signal control provides strong evidence of the algorithm's relevance.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** While the paper offers a novel approach using LLMs, the novelty is somewhat incremental, as LLMs have been explored in single agent settings. The strength of the results supports that there is merit in its implementation. The limited range of the validation and reliance on LLMs for curriculum generation hold it back from a higher rating. More expansive studies would boost its score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GDLLM: A Global Distance-aware Modeling Approach Based on Large Language Models for Event Temporal Relation Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20828v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Zhao, Wanting Ning, Yuxiao Fei, Yubo Feng, Lishuang Li
- **Abstract**: In Natural Language Processing(NLP), Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE) is to recognize the temporal relations of two events. Prior studies have noted the importance of language models for ETRE. However, the restricted pre-trained knowledge of Small Language Models(SLMs) limits their capability to handle minority class relations in imbalanced classification datasets. For Large Language Models(LLMs), researchers adopt manually designed prompts or instructions, which may introduce extra noise, leading to interference with the model's judgment of the long-distance dependencies between events. To address these issues, we propose GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach based on LLMs. We first present a distance-aware graph structure utilizing Graph Attention Network(GAT) to assist the LLMs in capturing long-distance dependency features. Additionally, we design a temporal feature learning paradigm based on soft inference to augment the identification of relations with a short-distance proximity band, which supplements the probabilistic information generated by LLMs into the multi-head attention mechanism. Since the global feature can be captured effectively, our framework substantially enhances the performance of minority relation classes and improves the overall learning ability. Experiments on two publicly available datasets, TB-Dense and MATRES, demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and a novelty/significance score.

**Summary**

The paper proposes GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach, for Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE) based on Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of using Small Language Models (SLMs) for ETRE due to their restricted pre-trained knowledge, which makes them less effective with imbalanced classification datasets containing minority classes. It also tackles the problem of manually designing prompts and instructions for LLMs, which can introduce noise and interfere with capturing long-distance dependencies between events. GDLLM utilizes a distance-aware graph structure with a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to capture long-distance dependency features. It also introduces a temporal feature learning paradigm based on soft inference, using the LLM’s probability distribution as edge features in the GAT, enriching the short-distance proximity information. Experiments on TB-Dense and MATRES datasets demonstrate that GDLLM achieves state-of-the-art performance without manually designed prompts or instructions. The method shows improved performance on minority relation classes, a common problem in ETRE.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Strengths:*

*   **Addressing Key Limitations:** The paper directly addresses significant challenges in ETRE: the limitations of SLMs and the drawbacks of manual prompt engineering for LLMs. These are acknowledged problems in the community.
*   **Novel Architecture:**  The combination of a distance-aware graph structure (GAT) with an LLM-based probabilistic edge feature is a reasonable approach. The use of the *probability distribution* rather than simply hard labels from the LLM as edge features ("soft inference") is a potential improvement.
*   **Performance Gains:** The experimental results show significant improvements over existing methods on standard datasets.  The ablation studies provide some insight into the contribution of individual components. The code will be available, which promotes reproducibility.
*   **Focus on Imbalanced Datasets:** The emphasis on handling imbalanced datasets and improving performance on minority classes is important, as this is a common issue in real-world ETRE scenarios.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The approach combines existing techniques (GATs and LLMs). The novelty lies primarily in how these components are integrated and fine-tuned for the specific ETRE task. While the performance improvements are notable, the core ideas are built on established methods. The "distance-aware" component is not particularly novel, as graph structures are inherently capable of representing distance.
*   **Limited Zero-Shot Evaluation:** The zero-shot results, while promising, don't fully demonstrate the generalizability. A broader range of datasets or task variations in zero-shot would significantly strengthen this aspect of the paper.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides empirical results, but lacks a solid theoretical analysis of why the proposed method works well.  For example, how the soft inference truly helps GAT to capture distance dependency.

*Significance:*

The paper has the potential to influence the ETRE research community by providing a more effective and automated approach to leverage LLMs. The improvements in handling imbalanced datasets are particularly valuable. However, the impact depends on the reproducibility of the results and the generalizability of the method to new datasets and scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a good combination of existing techniques to solve a significant problem in ETRE. The performance improvements are clear, and the ablation studies provide some insight into the contributions of different components. However, the approach is not radically new, as it mainly integrates established techniques. Morevoer, it lacks a bit theoretical justification. The significance lies in the practical improvements it offers for handling imbalanced datasets and reducing the need for manual prompt engineering.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Publish to Perish: Prompt Injection Attacks on LLM-Assisted Peer Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20863v1)
- **Authors**: Matteo Gioele Collu, Umberto Salviati, Roberto Confalonieri, Mauro Conti, Giovanni Apruzzese
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into the scientific peer-review process, raising new questions about their reliability and resilience to manipulation. In this work, we investigate the potential for hidden prompt injection attacks, where authors embed adversarial text within a paper's PDF to influence the LLM-generated review. We begin by formalising three distinct threat models that envision attackers with different motivations -- not all of which implying malicious intent. For each threat model, we design adversarial prompts that remain invisible to human readers yet can steer an LLM's output toward the author's desired outcome. Using a user study with domain scholars, we derive four representative reviewing prompts used to elicit peer reviews from LLMs. We then evaluate the robustness of our adversarial prompts across (i) different reviewing prompts, (ii) different commercial LLM-based systems, and (iii) different peer-reviewed papers. Our results show that adversarial prompts can reliably mislead the LLM, sometimes in ways that adversely affect a "honest-but-lazy" reviewer. Finally, we propose and empirically assess methods to reduce detectability of adversarial prompts under automated content checks.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential for hidden prompt injection attacks against Large Language Models (LLMs) when used in the scientific peer-review process. The authors formalize threat models, design adversarial prompts invisible to human readers, and evaluate their effectiveness in manipulating LLM-generated reviews across different LLMs and reviewing prompts. They propose methods to reduce the detectability of adversarial prompts and empirically assess their efficacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and timely concern about the integration of LLMs into peer review, an area that has not been thoroughly explored in terms of security and manipulation.
    *   **Formalization:** The threat model formalization provides a structured framework for understanding the different motivations and goals of attackers.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The extensive empirical evaluation using real-world papers, diverse LLMs, and different reviewing prompts provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed attacks.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The study highlights potential vulnerabilities that could compromise the integrity of the peer-review process and provides insights for developing effective defenses.
    *   **Countermeasures:** The investigation of countermeasures against detection demonstrates a proactive approach to addressing the identified vulnerabilities.
    *   **Ethics:** The authors emphasize not seeking to "break everything", cases in which attacks are not very effective should not be taken as weaknesses – rather, they confirm the fair nature of their research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Defenses:** While the paper explores some countermeasures, the scope of defenses investigated is relatively limited, and more research is needed to develop robust defenses against these attacks.
    *   **Generalizability:** The effectiveness of the attacks may vary depending on the specific LLMs and reviewing prompts used, and further research is needed to assess the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Black-Box Approach:** Given that commercial LLMs are used, a full understanding of the models' internal processes is not possible. The conclusions are solely based on empirical evidence.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it raises awareness about the potential for prompt injection attacks to undermine the integrity of LLM-assisted peer review. It calls for a renewed discussion on the pros and cons of using LLMs in the peer-review process, and offers insights for developing detection techniques and protective mechanisms. It calls for increased attention to LLM security in areas that could cause a significant impact if the vulnerabilities are ignored.

**Score: 8**

The paper is a high-quality and original work that makes a valuable contribution to the understanding of LLM security in the context of scientific peer review. The paper identifies a significant problem, provides a systematic analysis of the potential attacks, and offers practical insights for developing defenses. While the scope of defenses investigated could be expanded, the paper overall is a solid contribution with significant practical implications.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Deep Learning Framework for Early Detection of Pancreatic Cancer Using Multi-Modal Medical Imaging Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20877v1)
- **Authors**: Dennis Slobodzian, Karissa Tilbury, Amir Kordijazi
- **Abstract**: Pacreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) remains one of the most lethal forms of cancer, with a five-year survival rate below 10% primarily due to late detection. This research develops and validates a deep learning framework for early PDAC detection through analysis of dual-modality imaging: autofluorescence and second harmonic generation (SHG). We analyzed 40 unique patient samples to create a specialized neural network capable of distinguishing between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous tissue. Our methodology evaluated six distinct deep learning architectures, comparing traditional Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) with modern Vision Transformers (ViTs). Through systematic experimentation, we identified and overcome significant challenges in medical image analysis, including limited dataset size and class imbalance. The final optimized framework, based on a modified ResNet architecture with frozen pre-trained layers and class-weighted training, achieved over 90% accuracy in cancer detection. This represents a significant improvement over current manual analysis methods an demonstrates potential for clinical deployment. This work establishes a robust pipeline for automated PDAC detection that can augment pathologists' capabilities while providing a foundation for future expansion to other cancer types. The developed methodology also offers valuable insights for applying deep learning to limited-size medical imaging datasets, a common challenge in clinical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a deep learning framework for the early detection of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) using dual-modality autofluorescence and second harmonic generation (SHG) imaging. The study analyzes 40 patient samples, comparing Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) to distinguish between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous tissue.  The research addresses challenges such as limited dataset size and class imbalance through techniques like frozen pre-trained layers, class-weighted training, and data augmentation. The optimized ResNet-based framework achieves over 90% accuracy in cancer detection, demonstrating potential for clinical deployment and offering insights for applying deep learning to limited-size medical imaging datasets. The authors emphasize the use of explainable AI (Grad-CAM) to increase clinician trust and adoption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper has some novelty in its application of deep learning to PDAC detection using a specific combination of autofluorescence and SHG imaging. While deep learning and radiomics have been applied to PDAC detection using CT and MRI, the dual-modality approach presented here, though not entirely unexplored in the field, coupled with the specific techniques to overcome dataset limitations, contributes a novel perspective. The use of a frozen backbone and class-weighted training to mitigate overfitting and class imbalance are not revolutionary on their own, but their systematic application and optimization in this specific context adds value.  The adoption of Grad-CAM for interpretability is also a common practice, but still valuable for translational applications.

**Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve early PDAC detection, a critical factor for patient survival.  Demonstrating high accuracy in a constrained data environment is valuable, as it reflects the realities of many medical imaging applications.  The paper provides a robust pipeline and documented process that could be adapted for other cancer types or imaging modalities. The emphasis on interpretability through Grad-CAM is a positive step towards clinical acceptance.  However, the limited sample size and lack of external validation are significant weaknesses.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the problem of late PDAC detection and the limitations of existing diagnostic methods.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The approach is well-structured and clearly explained, detailing the imaging techniques, data preprocessing, deep learning architectures, and training strategies.
*   **Systematic Optimization:** The paper demonstrates a systematic approach to addressing challenges like overfitting and class imbalance.
*   **High Accuracy:** The reported accuracy is promising and suggests the potential for clinical application.
*   **Emphasis on Interpretability:**  The inclusion of Grad-CAM visualization is a valuable step towards increasing clinician trust and acceptance.
*   **Publicly available code:** Availability of the code increases reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The analysis is based on only 40 patient samples, which is a relatively small dataset for deep learning. This raises concerns about the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Lack of External Validation:** The model was not validated on an independent dataset, which is crucial for assessing its robustness and generalizability.
*   **Single-Center Study:** The data was collected from a single institution, which may introduce bias and limit the generalizability of the results.
*   **Modest Novelty:** While the specific application to dual-modality SHG and autofluorescence imaging has value, the individual deep learning techniques (frozen backbones, class weighting) are not ground-breaking on their own.
*   **Explainability Limitations:** While Grad-CAM improves interpretability, it does not guarantee a comprehensive understanding of the model's decision-making process. It is more of an indicator.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper provides a valuable starting point for further research in this area. The methodology and insights for addressing dataset limitations could be particularly useful for other medical imaging applications.  If validated in larger, multi-center studies, this approach could have a significant impact on PDAC diagnosis and patient outcomes.

**Score:** 6.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid technical approach with promising results, particularly given the limited dataset size. However, the lack of external validation and the incremental novelty in the methods restrict the overall impact.  While the application to dual-modality imaging has value, the contributions are more of an engineering and implementation nature rather than a fundamental breakthrough in deep learning. The small dataset, the single institution source, and lack of external validation are serious limitations that restrict the score to 6.5. While the code being publicly available is a strength, it can only be leveraged by the community with the data available. The adoption of explainable AI through Grad-CAM is beneficial, but it should not be a primary driver for increasing the score. Future studies with larger datasets and external validation are needed to confirm the generalizability and clinical utility of this approach.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Understanding and evaluating computer vision models through the lens of counterfactuals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20881v1)
- **Authors**: Pushkar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Counterfactual reasoning -- the practice of asking ``what if'' by varying inputs and observing changes in model behavior -- has become central to interpretable and fair AI. This thesis develops frameworks that use counterfactuals to explain, audit, and mitigate bias in vision classifiers and generative models. By systematically altering semantically meaningful attributes while holding others fixed, these methods uncover spurious correlations, probe causal dependencies, and help build more robust systems. The first part addresses vision classifiers. CAVLI integrates attribution (LIME) with concept-level analysis (TCAV) to quantify how strongly decisions rely on human-interpretable concepts. With localized heatmaps and a Concept Dependency Score, CAVLI shows when models depend on irrelevant cues like backgrounds. Extending this, ASAC introduces adversarial counterfactuals that perturb protected attributes while preserving semantics. Through curriculum learning, ASAC fine-tunes biased models for improved fairness and accuracy while avoiding stereotype-laden artifacts. The second part targets generative Text-to-Image (TTI) models. TIBET provides a scalable pipeline for evaluating prompt-sensitive biases by varying identity-related terms, enabling causal auditing of how race, gender, and age affect image generation. To capture interactions, BiasConnect builds causal graphs diagnosing intersectional biases. Finally, InterMit offers a modular, training-free algorithm that mitigates intersectional bias via causal sensitivity scores and user-defined fairness goals. Together, these contributions show counterfactuals as a unifying lens for interpretability, fairness, and causality in both discriminative and generative models, establishing principled, scalable methods for socially responsible bias evaluation and mitigation.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided thesis:

**Summary:**

The thesis addresses the critical challenges of fairness, interpretability, and robustness in computer vision and text-to-image (TTI) generative models. It advocates for counterfactual reasoning as a central tool for explaining model behavior, auditing biases, and implementing mitigation strategies. The work introduces several frameworks: (1) CAVLI, a method for quantifying the influence of visual concepts in classification via counterfactual intervention, (2) ASACs, a method for in-situ counterfactual generation using adversarial examples to mitigate biases in image classifiers, (3) TIBET, a pipeline for evaluating biases in TTI models by varying identity-related terms, (4) BiasConnect and BiasGraph, tools for measuring and visualizing intersectional effects between social attributes, and (5) InterMit, a modular, training-free algorithm for mitigating intersectional bias in TTI models using user-defined fairness goals. The thesis demonstrates these frameworks' effectiveness in understanding, evaluating, and mitigating biases in both vision classifiers and generative models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The thesis represents a significant and well-structured contribution to the field of AI safety and fairness, specifically concerning computer vision and generative modeling. Its key strength lies in its systematic approach to applying counterfactual reasoning across diverse tasks and model types, offering a unified framework for explanation, diagnosis, and mitigation of biases. The individual contributions, such as CAVLI, ASACs, TIBET, BiasConnect and InterMit each tackle a well-defined problem with well-justified methodologies and thorough evaluations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Framework:** The consistent use of counterfactual reasoning provides a unifying theme, connecting seemingly disparate problems (explainability in classifiers, bias mitigation in generative models).
*   **Novelty of Approaches:** The thesis introduces several novel techniques, such as ASACs for in-situ counterfactual generation, TIBET for dynamic bias evaluation, and BiasConnect/BiasGraph for quantifying intersectional effects. These techniques represent advancements over existing methods.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The frameworks are designed to be practically useful, addressing concrete challenges in real-world computer vision and generative modeling systems. The modularity and training-free nature of InterMit, in particular, increases its potential for adoption.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** The thesis acknowledges the ethical implications of bias in AI and proposes methods that aim to improve fairness and accountability. It explicitly addresses concerns around representational harm and the potential for misuse of mitigation techniques.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The frameworks are evaluated using a variety of datasets, metrics, and user studies, demonstrating their effectiveness in achieving their intended goals. The inclusion of user studies lends credibility to the human-interpretability of CAVLI's explanations and the alignment of TIBET with human judgment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Scalability:** The reliance on computationally intensive processes like counterfactual generation and VQA could limit the scalability of some frameworks to very large datasets or complex models.
*   **Dependence on VQA (TIBET):** The accuracy and biases of the VQA model could influence the evaluation results in TIBET, potentially leading to inaccurate assessments of bias. The authors acknowledge this limitation and address it through sensitivity analysis. However, further investigation into alternative image comparison techniques could be beneficial.
*   **Predefined Concepts (CAVLI):** CAVLI relies on human-defined concepts, limiting its ability to discover unexpected or emergent patterns of bias. Although the thesis acknowledges this limitation, the reliance on predefined concepts remains a potential constraint.
*   **Simplifications (InterMit):** InterMit simplifies complex social attributes such as gender and ethnicity into discrete, categorical variables, deviating from their experience in real life.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

This thesis has the potential to significantly influence the field of AI safety and fairness by providing practitioners with a suite of practical tools and a systematic approach to addressing bias in computer vision and generative models. Its emphasis on context-sensitive, dynamic evaluation and mitigation strategies could lead to the development of more robust, reliable, and equitable AI systems. The introduction of novel methods for quantifying and visualizing intersectional effects represents a particularly valuable contribution, opening up new avenues for research and development in this area.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**
The thesis demonstrates significant novelty in addressing a core set of AI safety related problems related to biases in computer vision tasks. However, there are a few limitations as discussed in the weaknesses. Overall, the thesis sets forth a well-reasoned contribution to the field that balances rigour with usability, while also acknowledging ethical nuances.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Lattice Random Walk Discretisations of Stochastic Differential Equations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20883v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Duffield, Maxwell Aifer, Denis Melanson, Zach Belateche, Patrick J. Coles
- **Abstract**: We introduce a lattice random walk discretisation scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs) that samples binary or ternary increments at each step, suppressing complex drift and diffusion computations to simple 1 or 2 bit random values. This approach is a significant departure from traditional floating point discretisations and offers several advantages; including compatibility with stochastic computing architectures that avoid floating-point arithmetic in place of directly manipulating the underlying probability distribution of a bitstream, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantisation errors, and handling of non-Lipschitz drifts. We prove weak convergence and demonstrate the advantages through experiments on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Lattice Random Walk (LRW) discretization scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs). This scheme significantly departs from traditional floating-point discretization methods by sampling binary or ternary increments at each step, effectively reducing complex computations to simple 1 or 2-bit random values. This approach offers several advantages, including compatibility with stochastic computing architectures, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantization errors, and the ability to handle non-Lipschitz drifts. The authors prove weak convergence of the LRW scheme and demonstrate its advantages through experiments on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel discretization scheme (LRW). While prior work (e.g., [37]) has explored related ideas like binary/ternary noise sources, LRW uniquely incorporates the drift term directly into the binary/ternary sampling process. This is a crucial distinction, especially concerning stochastic computing compatibility. The application of LRW to state-of-the-art diffusion models is also a novel demonstration of its scalability.

*   **Significance:** The LRW scheme has several potentially significant advantages:
    *   **Stochastic Computing:** The compatibility with stochastic computing architectures is perhaps the most significant contribution. This could unlock considerable speedups in SDE simulations, especially as specialized hardware is developed.
    *   **Robustness to Quantization:** The scheme's inherent robustness to quantization errors is highly relevant in the context of large models (e.g., diffusion models) where quantization is used for efficient computation. This could directly translate to reduced computational cost and energy consumption.
    *   **Handling Non-Lipschitz Drifts:** The stability of LRW in the face of non-Lipschitz drifts is also significant, as many practical SDEs do not satisfy the Lipschitz condition. This expands the applicability of numerical SDE solvers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-motivated description of the LRW scheme.
    *   The weak convergence proof provides theoretical support for the method's accuracy.
    *   The experimental results effectively demonstrate the advantages of LRW, especially its robustness to quantization and its stability for non-Lipschitz drifts. The diffusion model results show real-world applicability.
    *   The discussion section is thorough, acknowledging limitations and outlining future directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The restriction to diagonal diffusion matrices is a significant limitation, although the authors address it and propose potential solutions.
    *   The weak convergence proof only establishes order 1 accuracy. While this is comparable to Euler-Maruyama, higher-order schemes are available.
    *   The paper doesn't include direct experiments on stochastic computing hardware. This is understandable given the complexity of such experiments, but it limits the empirical validation of this key advantage.
    *   The advantages stemming from the lack of Gaussian sampling and from robustness to quantization are experimentally shown, but the quantitative gain in performance of large SDEs remains unclear.

*   **Potential Influence:** The LRW scheme has the potential to influence several areas:
    *   **Stochastic Computing:** It could spur the development of specialized stochastic computing hardware for SDE simulation.
    *   **Diffusion Models:** It could provide a more efficient and robust alternative to existing discretization methods, especially for quantized models.
    *   **General SDE Solvers:** It could serve as a stable and accurate method for solving SDEs with non-Lipschitz drifts.

Overall, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of numerical SDE solvers. The LRW scheme offers a novel approach with several potential advantages, especially in the context of stochastic computing and quantized models. While the limitations (diagonal diffusion matrices, order 1 accuracy) should be addressed in future work, the paper provides a strong foundation for further research and development.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

A score of 8 reflects the paper's genuine novelty and potential significance, tempered by the existing limitations. The LRW scheme's compatibility with stochastic computing, if fully realized through dedicated hardware, could be transformative. The robustness to quantization is practically important. However, the restriction to diagonal diffusion and lack of direct hardware experiments prevent a higher score. The paper presents a solid theoretical and experimental foundation, making it a strong contribution warranting a score above average.

- **Classification**: math.NA
- **Score**: 8/10

### PromptSleuth: Detecting Prompt Injection via Semantic Intent Invariance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20890v1)
- **Authors**: Mengxiao Wang, Yuxuan Zhang, Guofei Gu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into real-world applications, from virtual assistants to autonomous agents. However, their flexibility also introduces new attack vectors-particularly Prompt Injection (PI), where adversaries manipulate model behavior through crafted inputs. As attackers continuously evolve with paraphrased, obfuscated, and even multi-task injection strategies, existing benchmarks are no longer sufficient to capture the full spectrum of emerging threats. To address this gap, we construct a new benchmark that systematically extends prior efforts. Our benchmark subsumes the two widely-used existing ones while introducing new manipulation techniques and multi-task scenarios, thereby providing a more comprehensive evaluation setting. We find that existing defenses, though effective on their original benchmarks, show clear weaknesses under our benchmark, underscoring the need for more robust solutions. Our key insight is that while attack forms may vary, the adversary's intent-injecting an unauthorized task-remains invariant. Building on this observation, we propose PromptSleuth, a semantic-oriented defense framework that detects prompt injection by reasoning over task-level intent rather than surface features. Evaluated across state-of-the-art benchmarks, PromptSleuth consistently outperforms existing defense while maintaining comparable runtime and cost efficiency. These results demonstrate that intent-based semantic reasoning offers a robust, efficient, and generalizable strategy for defending LLMs against evolving prompt injection threats.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PromptSleuth, a new semantic-oriented defense framework designed to detect prompt injection attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs). Prompt injection attacks manipulate LLMs by embedding adversarial instructions within user inputs.  The core idea is that while attack forms may vary significantly, the attacker's *intent* to inject an unauthorized task remains constant. PromptSleuth leverages this invariant by analyzing the semantic relationship between the intended task and the injected task, reasoning over task-level intent rather than surface-level features. The paper also presents PromptSleuth-Bench, a new benchmark dataset that extends prior benchmarks with diversified attack variants and multi-task scenarios.  Evaluations show that PromptSleuth outperforms existing defenses on this benchmark while maintaining comparable runtime and cost efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*  The paper's novelty lies primarily in shifting the focus of prompt injection defense from surface-level pattern matching to semantic intent analysis.  Existing defenses often rely on keyword filters, grammar heuristics, or classifiers trained on known injection examples, which are easily bypassed by evolving attack variants. PromptSleuth offers a more robust approach by analyzing the underlying task intent, reasoning that the attacker's goal remains consistent even as attack methods change. This insight is valuable and represents a non-trivial departure from existing defensive strategies. Constructing PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH is another valuable effort, offering a more realistic and rigorous evaluation environment.  Furthermore, the categorization of prompt injection attacks into system prompt forgery, user prompt camouflage, and model behavior manipulation helps clarify the problem space and explains why prior defenses fail. The incorporation of multi-task scenarios is also innovative.

*Significance:* The paper's significance is high due to the increasing importance of LLMs in real-world applications and the growing threat of prompt injection attacks. By demonstrating the limitations of existing defenses and offering a more effective and generalizable solution, PromptSleuth contributes significantly to the security of LLM-based systems. The paper's claim of comparable runtime and cost efficiency is also crucial for practical deployment.  The open-sourcing of the benchmark and defense framework is a further strength, facilitating reproducibility and encouraging wider adoption.

*Strengths:*

*   **Strong Conceptual Foundation:** The paper's core insight about invariant attacker intent provides a solid foundation for the defense framework.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The PromptSleuth-Bench dataset offers a more realistic and challenging evaluation environment compared to existing benchmarks.
*   **Superior Performance:**  The evaluation results demonstrate that PromptSleuth outperforms existing defenses on a challenging benchmark.
*   **Practical Considerations:** The paper addresses practical considerations such as runtime, cost efficiency, and compatibility with different LLM providers.
*   **Open Source Release:**  Releasing the benchmark and defense framework promotes reproducibility and adoption.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Dependence on LLM Capabilities:** PromptSleuth's performance is heavily dependent on the underlying LLM's ability to perform task summarization and semantic reasoning. As the ablation study reveals, the performance can degrade with weaker LLMs. The claims made about being resistant to model evolution and relying on general-purpose LLMs should be tested with future generation of LLMs and their vulnerabilities to new kinds of attacks and vulnerabilities.
*   **Complexity:** The framework involves multiple LLM calls, which can increase latency and cost. While the paper claims comparable efficiency, further optimization may be necessary for real-time applications. The potential is that attacks may emerge that are so subtle, no LLM can detect it and, thereby, the framework will fail
*   **Limited Evaluation Scenarios:** The evaluation focuses primarily on relatively simple tasks. The framework's performance on more complex, interactive, and tool-augmented LLM agents may differ and needs to be further explored.  The multi-task scenarios, while an improvement, could be made even more complex and realistic.
*   **Lack of Comparative Analysis:**  Although the paper compares against SOTA, it provides limited detailed analysis of where specific improvements are achieved. A more in-depth per-attack analysis could be done to demonstrate where PromptSleuth excels.

*Potential Influence:* PromptSleuth has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM security by promoting a shift towards semantic-oriented defenses.  The PromptSleuth-Bench dataset can serve as a valuable resource for future research and development.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, I would assign a score of 7.

While PromptSleuth offers a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM security, several aspects prevent it from achieving a higher score. The dependence on LLM capabilities, while a strength in leveraging advancements, also introduces a potential point of failure. The framework's complexity and cost considerations, as well as the limited evaluation scenarios, suggest that further development and optimization are necessary. The lack of detailed comparative analysis prevents a deep understanding.

The paper successfully addresses a crucial problem with a novel solution, offering demonstrable improvements over existing approaches. However, the limitations must be addressed before PromptSleuth can be considered a truly transformative contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Uneven Impact of Post-Training Quantization in Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20893v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Marie, Atsushi Fujita
- **Abstract**: Quantization is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained hardware, but its implications for multilingual tasks remain underexplored. We conduct the first large-scale evaluation of post-training quantization (PTQ) on machine translation across 55 languages using five LLMs ranging from 1.7B to 70B parameters. Our analysis reveals that while 4-bit quantization often preserves translation quality for high-resource languages and large models, significant degradation occurs for low-resource and typologically diverse languages, particularly in 2-bit settings. We compare four quantization techniques (AWQ, BitsAndBytes, GGUF, and AutoRound), showing that algorithm choice and model size jointly determine robustness. GGUF variants provide the most consistent performance, even at 2-bit precision. Additionally, we quantify the interactions between quantization, decoding hyperparameters, and calibration languages, finding that language-matched calibration offers benefits primarily in low-bit scenarios. Our findings offer actionable insights for deploying multilingual LLMs for machine translation under quantization constraints, especially in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of post-training quantization (PTQ) on machine translation (MT) performance across a diverse set of languages and language models. It examines five LLMs (ranging from 1.7B to 70B parameters) quantized using four techniques (AWQ, BitsAndBytes, GGUF, AutoRound) and evaluates translation quality on 55 languages. Key findings include: 1) Quantization degrades performance more severely for smaller models and low-resource languages; 2) GGUF quantization provides more consistent performance, especially at lower bitwidths (2-bit); and 3) Language-matched calibration can improve performance in low-bit scenarios. The study also analyzes the effects of decoding hyperparameters (temperature, nucleus sampling).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents the first large-scale, systematic evaluation of PTQ on MT across a substantial number of languages and models.  While prior work has explored quantization in multilingual settings, they often lack the scope and breadth of this study. The analysis of various quantization methods and the identification of GGUF's robustness is also a valuable contribution. However, the individual quantization techniques and the general concept of quantization are not novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in understanding the real-world deployability of multilingual LLMs. Quantization is crucial for resource-constrained environments, and the paper's insights into the language-specific effects are highly relevant. Identifying GGUF as a robust quantization technique for a wide variety of languages is extremely helpful. The observations about the impact of decoding hyperparameters, while not entirely surprising, provides guidance for practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Large-scale and comprehensive evaluation covering many languages, models, and quantization techniques.
    *   Clear and well-organized presentation of results.
    *   Practical insights for deploying multilingual LLMs in low-resource settings.
    *   Detailed analysis of the interaction between quantization, language resource level, and algorithmic choices.
    *   Addresses the nuances of applying techniques often tested mostly on English to multilingual contexts.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Evaluation is limited to automatic metrics (COMET). While COMET is a decent metric, it cannot replace human evaluation for translation quality. It's also not calibrated across languages, weakening comparisons.
    *   The selection of languages, while diverse, could benefit from a more rigorous justification based on typological features or other linguistic criteria. The paper mentions "script diversity", which is not a well-defined concept.
    *   The study focuses only on weight-only PTQ. While justified, exploring activation quantization could offer a more complete picture.
    *   The hyperparameter search for quantization and inference is limited. There may be better settings for each language and model that are not explored.

*   **Rigorous Rationale**
The study does an excellent job of covering the different quantization approaches for MT. By combining different LLMs with a breadth of languages, it provides a useful result that demonstrates which models can be used on hardware with smaller computing power. The main downfall, however, is the lack of human evaluations, and the study could do more to improve on this, or mention it as an area for future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on quantization for multilingual NLP. It provides a valuable benchmark and highlights the importance of considering language diversity when developing and evaluating compression techniques. The identification of GGUF's robustness and the insights into decoding hyperparameters will be useful for practitioners deploying multilingual LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**
The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of PTQ on MT, addressing an important gap in the literature. The large-scale analysis, clear presentation, and practical insights contribute significantly to the field. However, the reliance on automatic metrics and the limited hyperparameter search weaken the conclusions somewhat. The novelty is good, but the fundamental work isn't new. Overall, the paper offers a valuable and well-executed study that should influence future research and practice in multilingual NLP.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Language-Enhanced Mobile Manipulation for Efficient Object Search in Indoor Environments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20899v1)
- **Authors**: Liding Zhang, Zeqi Li, Kuanqi Cai, Qian Huang, Zhenshan Bing, Alois Knoll
- **Abstract**: Enabling robots to efficiently search for and identify objects in complex, unstructured environments is critical for diverse applications ranging from household assistance to industrial automation. However, traditional scene representations typically capture only static semantics and lack interpretable contextual reasoning, limiting their ability to guide object search in completely unfamiliar settings. To address this challenge, we propose a language-enhanced hierarchical navigation framework that tightly integrates semantic perception and spatial reasoning. Our method, Goal-Oriented Dynamically Heuristic-Guided Hierarchical Search (GODHS), leverages large language models (LLMs) to infer scene semantics and guide the search process through a multi-level decision hierarchy. Reliability in reasoning is achieved through the use of structured prompts and logical constraints applied at each stage of the hierarchy. For the specific challenges of mobile manipulation, we introduce a heuristic-based motion planner that combines polar angle sorting with distance prioritization to efficiently generate exploration paths. Comprehensive evaluations in Isaac Sim demonstrate the feasibility of our framework, showing that GODHS can locate target objects with higher search efficiency compared to conventional, non-semantic search strategies. Website and Video are available at: https://drapandiger.github.io/GODHS
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GODHS (Goal-Oriented Dynamically Heuristic-Guided Hierarchical Search), a novel framework for language-enhanced mobile manipulation in indoor environments. It addresses the challenge of efficient object search in unfamiliar settings by integrating semantic perception and spatial reasoning through a hierarchical decision-making process. GODHS leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to infer scene semantics and guide search, using structured prompts and logical constraints to ensure reliability.  A heuristic-based motion planner combines polar angle sorting and distance prioritization for efficient path generation and exploration.  Simulations in Isaac Sim demonstrate that GODHS outperforms non-semantic search strategies in locating target objects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the *integrated* approach of combining LLMs with a hierarchical search strategy *specifically* designed for mobile manipulation. While LLMs have been used in robotics before, GODHS attempts to structure the LLM's reasoning within a well-defined hierarchical framework, applying constraints at each level to improve reliability. The heuristic-based motion planner, combining polar angle and lexicographic sorting, offers a practical contribution to pose planning within the search context. However, each part of this framework by itself is not completely new. The hierarchical structure has been explored in the past, LLMs are relatively new to robotics, and heuristic motion planning is well-established. It's the combination and adaptation of these elements to solve the specific object search problem that constitutes the primary novelty.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical and important problem: enabling robots to efficiently search for objects in complex environments.  Efficient object search is a critical capability for various robotics applications (e.g., assistive robotics, logistics). The use of LLMs to incorporate common-sense knowledge into the search process has the potential to significantly improve the performance of robots operating in unstructured settings. The paper shows initial promising results in simulation. A significant factor affecting the potential impact is the reliance on large language models. The demonstrated improvement stems directly from the reasoning capabilities of the LLM. Therefore, the paper's value is strongly linked to the continued development and accessibility of such models.

* **Strengths:**
    * The hierarchical decomposition of the search problem is a well-structured and intuitive approach.
    * The integration of LLMs to guide the search process based on semantic understanding is a valuable contribution.
    * The heuristic motion planner shows promise for generating efficient exploration paths.
    *  The results demonstrate a clear improvement over baseline methods in a simulated environment.
    * The use of structured prompts and logical constraints addresses the reliability issues that can arise from using LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The experiments are limited to a simulated environment. Real-world environments are significantly more complex and introduce additional challenges (e.g., noisy sensor data, dynamic environments).
    * The reliance on LLMs could be a bottleneck due to computational cost and API availability.
    * The paper does not provide a thorough analysis of the limitations of the approach (e.g., scenarios where GODHS fails). The "Failure Modes" section is very brief.
    * The evaluation could be more robust. A comparison to state-of-the-art methods that *also* leverage LLMs would be beneficial.
    * It is not completely clear how well GODHS would scale to different environments (e.g., very large or highly cluttered spaces).
    * The weights chosen for OSR might not be representative across multiple environments.

* **Potential Influence:** If GODHS can be successfully deployed on physical robots and adapted to real-world environments, it could significantly impact how robots perform object search and manipulation tasks. It provides a direction for how LLMs can be meaningfully integrated into robotic systems for semantic reasoning.

* **Score Rationale:**

Considering the novelty and significance, I would rate this paper a **6**. The *integration* of LLMs into the hierarchical search architecture tailored for mobile manipulation has merit. The experiments confirm the feasibility of this combination, highlighting improvements over baseline methods. The limitations relating to real-world validation and dependency on LLMs, along with potentially better comparisons could further improve the work.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Research Challenges in Relational Database Management Systems for LLM Queries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20912v1)
- **Authors**: Kerem Akillioglu, Anurag Chakraborty, Sairaj Voruganti, M. Tamer Özsu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have become essential for applications such as text summarization, sentiment analysis, and automated question-answering. Recently, LLMs have also been integrated into relational database management systems to enhance querying and support advanced data processing. Companies such as Amazon, Databricks, Google, and Snowflake offer LLM invocation directly within SQL, denoted as LLM queries, to boost data insights. However, open-source solutions currently have limited functionality and poor performance. In this work, we present an early exploration of two open-source systems and one enterprise platform, using five representative queries to expose functional, performance, and scalability limits in today's SQL-invoked LLM integrations. We identify three main issues: enforcing structured outputs, optimizing resource utilization, and improving query planning. We implemented initial solutions and observed improvements in accommodating LLM powered SQL queries. These early gains demonstrate that tighter integration of LLM+DBMS is the key to scalable and efficient processing of LLM queries.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the challenges of integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into Relational Database Management Systems (DBMSs) to enable SQL queries that directly invoke LLM capabilities (LLM queries).  It evaluates two open-source systems (PostgreSQL with pgAI and DuckDB with FlockMTL) and an enterprise solution (MotherDuck) using a set of representative LLM queries. The authors identify limitations in existing systems related to enforcing structured outputs, optimizing resource utilization (specifically GPU), and query planning. They implement initial solutions to address some of these challenges and observe improvements. The paper concludes that tighter integration of LLMs and DBMSs is crucial for scalable and efficient processing of LLM queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its early exploration of the practical challenges encountered when combining LLMs with DBMSs in an open-source context. While the *idea* of using LLMs with databases isn't entirely new (as acknowledged by the authors citing works from Amazon, Databricks, Snowflake, etc.), this paper goes beyond simply stating the concept.  It *demonstrates* the functional and performance limitations of existing, readily available solutions (pgAI, FlockMTL). The identification of specific issues like structured output problems, poor resource utilization with simple batching strategies, and planner limitations is a tangible contribution. It also provides a comparison between enterprise solutions and open source.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and growing area. The integration of AI, particularly LLMs, into data management systems is likely to become increasingly important. By highlighting the current challenges, the paper provides valuable insights and directions for future research and development. The focus on *open-source* solutions is especially significant, as it empowers a broader community to experiment with and contribute to this emerging field. The investigation into enterprise systems vs open source is also very relevant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inefficient and functionally limited LLM+DBMS integration.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The evaluation using multiple systems and a defined set of queries provides concrete evidence to support the claims.  The performance numbers and GPU utilization graphs are valuable.
    *   **Identification of Specific Challenges:**  The breakdown of challenges into structured outputs, resource utilization, and query planning makes the issues actionable.
    *   **Practical Insights:**  The initial solutions implemented and the observed improvements offer practical guidance.
    *   **Clear Future Directions:** The "Research Challenges" section explicitly outlines areas for future work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Solutions:**  The solutions implemented are initial and not fully comprehensive. For example, the batching optimizations may need further work.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While acknowledged and understandable due to computational cost, the relatively small dataset size (17,000 rows as the limit) might raise questions about the generalizability of the findings to truly large-scale data. Although, this is reasonable given the task.
    *   **Some Claims Need Stronger Support:** Some statements are a bit vague and would benefit from more supporting evidence. For instance, the specific details of the "manual enforcement of the optimal query plan" in pgAI could be elaborated on.
    *   **RAG query results** The paper claims that PGai is the only system able to execute the RAG query with manual plan enforcement. No further explanation is provided on why that is the case.
    *   **LLaMA 3 vs older LLMs** Given the advancements in LLMs from the beginning of this paper to now, it would be useful to see the same queries evaluated with more current models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:

    *   **Guiding Research:** Providing a roadmap for research on LLM query optimization, structured output handling, and resource management.
    *   **Informing System Design:**  Influencing the design of future LLM+DBMS systems, particularly open-source solutions.
    *   **Sparking Community Discussion:**  Encouraging discussion and collaboration within the database and AI communities.
    *   **Highlighting the importance** of tightly coupled systems as LLM queries become more prevalent

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a valuable initial investigation of a practically important problem. While the solutions presented are preliminary and the experimental setup has limitations, the paper's contribution lies in *clearly articulating the specific challenges* encountered when integrating LLMs into open-source DBMSs and providing an initial empirical evaluation. It takes the discussion beyond a theoretical level by showing where existing tools fall short. This is significant because it provides a concrete starting point for future research and development efforts. The investigation into the enterprise system vs open source is also important for future researchers.

The primary reasons for not scoring it higher are the relatively small dataset and limited scope of the solutions. The novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking, building on existing work in LLM integration but focusing on the open-source aspect. However, the clarity of the problem definition and the practical insights gained from the evaluation make it a worthwhile contribution that will likely influence future work in the field.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### SageLM: A Multi-aspect and Explainable Large Language Model for Speech Judgement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20916v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Ge, Junxiang Zhang, Xiaoqian Liu, Bei Li, Xiangnan Ma, Chenglong Wang, Kaiyang Ye, Yangfan Du, Linfeng Zhang, Yuxin Huang, Tong Xiao, Zhengtao Yu, JingBo Zhu
- **Abstract**: Speech-to-Speech (S2S) Large Language Models (LLMs) are foundational to natural human-computer interaction, enabling end-to-end spoken dialogue systems. However, evaluating these models remains a fundamental challenge. We propose \texttt{SageLM}, an end-to-end, multi-aspect, and explainable speech LLM for comprehensive S2S LLMs evaluation. First, unlike cascaded approaches that disregard acoustic features, SageLM jointly assesses both semantic and acoustic dimensions. Second, it leverages rationale-based supervision to enhance explainability and guide model learning, achieving superior alignment with evaluation outcomes compared to rule-based reinforcement learning methods. Third, we introduce \textit{SpeechFeedback}, a synthetic preference dataset, and employ a two-stage training paradigm to mitigate the scarcity of speech preference data. Trained on both semantic and acoustic dimensions, SageLM achieves an 82.79\% agreement rate with human evaluators, outperforming cascaded and SLM-based baselines by at least 7.42\% and 26.20\%, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SageLMS: A Multi-aspect and Explainable Large Language Model for Speech Judgement":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SageLM, a novel end-to-end, multi-aspect, and explainable speech LLM designed for comprehensive evaluation of Speech-to-Speech (S2S) LLMs.  It addresses the limitations of cascaded ASR-based evaluation methods by directly processing speech and considering both semantic and acoustic dimensions.  Key contributions include: (1) SpeechFeedback, a large-scale synthetic preference dataset for training speech evaluators; (2) Demonstration that fine-tuning on explicit rationales outperforms rule-based RL in judgment tasks; and (3)  SageLM itself, which achieves state-of-the-art performance in S2S dialogue evaluation, exhibiting high agreement with human evaluators. The model is trained using a two-stage SFT approach which first trains on semantic preferences and then acoustic ones.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty on multiple fronts:
    *   **End-to-End Speech Evaluation:** Bypassing ASR is a key step forward, mitigating error propagation and enabling consideration of acoustic features, which are ignored by text-based LLM evaluators.
    *   **SpeechFeedback Dataset:** Constructing a comprehensive dataset specifically for speech preference learning is valuable, given the scarcity of existing resources. The generation of synthetic data to provide a strong basis for evaluation provides a useful method for expanding datasets.
    *   **Rationale-Based Training:** Employing supervised fine-tuning with human-annotated rationales is a clever way to improve explainability and force the model to learn more consistent and understandable evaluation strategies.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a critical bottleneck in the development of S2S systems - the lack of robust, scalable evaluation methods.
    *   **Scalable Evaluation:** SageLM offers a path to more efficient and scalable evaluation, enabling rapid iteration and benchmarking of S2S models.
    *   **Multi-Aspect Judgment:** Incorporating both semantic and acoustic dimensions enables a more holistic evaluation than previous approaches.
    *   **Explainability:** The ability of SageLM to provide rationales for its judgments enhances trust and provides insights into model behavior. The improved performance is well-demonstrated in the experiments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper thoroughly evaluates SageLM against strong baselines, including cascaded approaches and other S2T LLMs.
    *   **Well-Defined Contributions:** The contributions are clearly articulated and supported by experimental results.
    *   **Address a Critical Problem:** Solves the problem of scalability in the evaluation of S2S systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Data Dependency:**  The SpeechFeedback dataset relies heavily on synthetic data generation. While this addresses data scarcity, the reliance on synthetic data could introduce biases or limitations that don't reflect real-world spoken dialogue. The model is only as good as the data, and there is no evaluation of the quality of the speech to determine if the generated synthetic data is reliable enough for real evaluations.
    *   **Limited to Textual Queries:** While the argument for textual queries promoting a fair evaluation is sound, it does limit the real-world use case of an S2S evaluator. In reality, real evaluation will require both speech to speech and text to speech evaluation, so the paper does not provide for the former.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Human Evaluation:** While the paper uses agreement with human evaluators as a key metric, the evaluation doesn't deeply analyze the disagreement cases. A qualitative analysis of instances where SageLM disagreed with humans could provide valuable insights.
    *   **Lack of information on the number of manually verified instances in the test set.** This would help provide information on the reliability of the evaluation.
    *   **No ablation studies of each aspect considered, so unclear if all aspects are important or some aspects are more important than others.**

*   **Potential Influence:** SageLM has the potential to significantly influence the development and evaluation of S2S systems. It provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners in the field. It could drive further research into end-to-end evaluation methods, rationale-based training, and multi-aspect judgment in speech dialogue systems.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper presents a solid contribution with clear novelty and significance, there are limitations in the model's reliance on synthetic data. Also, the paper is somewhat preliminary, and the limited evaluations (e.g., focusing just on textual query) make a high score unwarranted. Considering all these factors, the following score is justified:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Can Input Reformulation Improve Tool Usage Accuracy in a Complex Dynamic Environment? A Study on $τ$-bench
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20931v1)
- **Authors**: Venkatesh Mishra, Amir Saeidi, Satyam Raj, Mutsumi Nakamura, Jayanth Srinivasa, Gaowen Liu, Ali Payani, Chitta Baral
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in reasoning and planning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) have enabled their potential as autonomous agents capable of tool use in dynamic environments. However, in multi-turn conversational environments like $\tau$-bench, these agents often struggle with consistent reasoning, adherence to domain-specific policies, and extracting correct information over a long horizon of tool-calls and conversation. To capture and mitigate these failures, we conduct a comprehensive manual analysis of the common errors occurring in the conversation trajectories. We then experiment with reformulations of inputs to the tool-calling agent for improvement in agent decision making. Finally, we propose the Input-Reformulation Multi-Agent (IRMA) framework, which automatically reformulates user queries augmented with relevant domain rules and tool suggestions for the tool-calling agent to focus on. The results show that IRMA significantly outperforms ReAct, Function Calling, and Self-Reflection by 16.1%, 12.7%, and 19.1%, respectively, in overall pass^5 scores. These findings highlight the superior reliability and consistency of IRMA compared to other methods in dynamic environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "How Can Input Reformulation Improve Tool Usage Accuracy in a Complex Dynamic Environment? A Study on T-bench" addresses the challenges faced by large language models (LLMs) when acting as autonomous agents in complex, multi-turn conversational environments. It focuses on improving the accuracy and reliability of tool usage, particularly in scenarios like the T-bench benchmark, which simulates airline and retail dialogues. The authors propose Input-Reformulation Multi-Agent (IRMA) framework, which reformulates user queries by augmenting them with relevant domain rules and tool suggestions. IRMA aims to provide structured, contextually rich inputs to the LLM agent, enabling it to make more accurate decisions and adhere to domain-specific policies. The paper presents a detailed error classification for LLM failures in tool-calling and demonstrates that IRMA outperforms existing methods like ReAct, Function Calling, and Self-Reflection in terms of accuracy, reliability, and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Error Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed error classification of LLM failures in multi-turn tool-calling scenarios, offering a structured understanding of the problem space. This is a valuable contribution in itself.
    *   **Novel Framework (IRMA):**  The IRMA framework is a novel approach that focuses on improving the *input* to the agent, rather than relying on post-hoc correction or verification, as is common in other methods. This is a potentially more efficient and effective strategy.
