Because errors can incur costly penalties, any automated system must deliver high accuracy and auditability, making modern large language models (LLMs) poorly suited for this task. We propose an approach that integrates LLMs with a symbolic solver to calculate tax obligations. We evaluate variants of this system on the challenging StAtutory Reasoning Assessment (SARA) dataset, and include a novel method for estimating the cost of deploying such a system based on real-world penalties for tax errors. We further show how combining up-front translation of plain-text rules into formal logic programs, combined with intelligently retrieved exemplars for formal case representations, can dramatically improve performance on this task and reduce costs to well below real-world averages. Our results demonstrate the promise and economic feasibility of neuro-symbolic architectures for increasing equitable access to reliable tax assistance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of equitable access to trustworthy financial reasoning, particularly in the context of tax filing. It argues that while large language models (LLMs) have potential in this domain, their lack of accuracy, auditability, and inability to recognize limitations hinder their suitability for tasks with high-stakes consequences like tax calculations.  The authors propose a neuro-symbolic approach that integrates LLMs with a symbolic solver (Prolog) to calculate tax obligations. They evaluate this system on the StAtutory Reasoning Assessment (SARA) dataset and introduce a novel cost estimation method based on real-world tax error penalties. The key findings are: (1) frontier reasoning models outperform non-reasoning models at parsing statutory text, and (2) adding symbolic solver refusal criteria and self-checking significantly reduces deployment costs. The results suggest that this neuro-symbolic architecture can provide more reliable and affordable tax assistance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the *specific* combination of techniques for the *tax domain*. While neuro-symbolic approaches and the use of symbolic solvers with LLMs are not entirely novel, their application to the SARA dataset and the integration with intelligent exemplar retrieval represent a unique contribution. The cost estimation method based on tax error penalties is a valuable practical addition. The intelligent re-ranking strategy is another significant point that helps improve the system's trustworthiness by providing appropriate precedent examples. The self-consistency tests also address the critical issue of trustworthiness.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a critical real-world problem: the complexity and cost of tax filing, which disproportionately affects lower-income communities. By demonstrating the feasibility of an AI-assisted system that can provide accurate, auditable, and affordable tax advice, the paper contributes to the broader goal of equitable access to essential services. The SARA dataset evaluation grounds the significance by providing a robust benchmark, while the cost analysis translates technical results into economic benefits. Furthermore, this paper offers a novel understanding of the different strengths and weaknesses exhibited by reasoning- and chat-optimized models in the tax domain.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of applying LLMs to tax reasoning.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The experiments on the SARA dataset are thorough and comprehensive.
    *   **Practical cost analysis:** The inclusion of a cost estimation based on real-world tax penalties provides a practical dimension.
    *   **Clear results:** The paper effectively communicates the benefits of the neuro-symbolic architecture and the importance of intelligent exemplar retrieval.
    *   **Strong discussion:** The discussion offers valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different models and methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While SARA is a valuable benchmark, it is a *synthetic* dataset. Performance on a real-world dataset of tax returns would further strengthen the findings.
    *   **Translation Bottleneck:** The requirement of manually translating statutes and cases into Prolog remains a significant bottleneck for widespread adoption.
    *   **Scope of Tax Law:** The SARA dataset covers a limited scope of the US tax code. The scalability of the approach to more complex and comprehensive tax scenarios needs further investigation. The dependency on gold statute translation further limits the generalizability of this approach.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed approach to a critical real-world problem. The neuro-symbolic architecture, combined with the cost estimation and intelligent exemplar retrieval, represents a significant step towards more reliable and affordable tax assistance. While the limitations of the dataset and the translation bottleneck prevent a higher score, the paper's novelty, significance, and rigorous evaluation justify a strong rating.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture of Contexts for Long Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21058v1)
- **Authors**: Shengqu Cai, Ceyuan Yang, Lvmin Zhang, Yuwei Guo, Junfei Xiao, Ziyan Yang, Yinghao Xu, Zhenheng Yang, Alan Yuille, Leonidas Guibas, Maneesh Agrawala, Lu Jiang, Gordon Wetzstein
- **Abstract**: Long video generation is fundamentally a long context memory problem: models must retain and retrieve salient events across a long range without collapsing or drifting. However, scaling diffusion transformers to generate long-context videos is fundamentally limited by the quadratic cost of self-attention, which makes memory and computation intractable and difficult to optimize for long sequences. We recast long-context video generation as an internal information retrieval task and propose a simple, learnable sparse attention routing module, Mixture of Contexts (MoC), as an effective long-term memory retrieval engine. In MoC, each query dynamically selects a few informative chunks plus mandatory anchors (caption, local windows) to attend to, with causal routing that prevents loop closures. As we scale the data and gradually sparsify the routing, the model allocates compute to salient history, preserving identities, actions, and scenes over minutes of content. Efficiency follows as a byproduct of retrieval (near-linear scaling), which enables practical training and synthesis, and the emergence of memory and consistency at the scale of minutes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Mixture of Contexts for Long Video Generation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of long-context video generation, which is limited by the quadratic complexity of self-attention in diffusion transformers (DiTs). The authors propose a "Mixture of Contexts" (MoC) module, a learnable sparse attention routing mechanism. MoC treats long video generation as an internal information retrieval problem. It dynamically selects relevant chunks of video and caption tokens for attention, avoiding the need for dense self-attention across the entire sequence. A learned routing policy focuses compute on salient historical context, preserving identities and actions across extended durations. The method includes causal masking to prevent loop closures, and progressive sparsification during training. The authors demonstrate improved efficiency (near-linear scaling), enabling minute-scale video generation while maintaining or improving fidelity compared to dense attention baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using sparse attention for long video sequences is not entirely new, as the paper itself acknowledges (citing prior work on keyframe selection, frame packs, static sparsity, etc.). However, the *learnable* and *adaptive* routing approach of MoC is a significant contribution. The combination of content-aligned chunking, explicit cross-modal and intra-shot links, causal masking, and the progressive sparsification strategy is a novel design.

*   **Significance:** Overcoming the quadratic complexity of self-attention is a major bottleneck in scaling up video generation. MoC offers a practical solution that makes minute-long video generation more feasible. The fact that the method can maintain or improve fidelity compared to dense attention while being more efficient is a significant result. The learned routing policy, by focusing compute on salient history, allows for better preservation of identities and consistency across shots. The method also enables text-guided video editing, proving to be effective in handling multimodal tasks as well as long context tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Effective Sparsification:**  The MoC module achieves significant sparsification (e.g., 85% reduction in attention pairs) leading to considerable FLOPs reduction.
    *   **Improved Efficiency:** The claimed 2.2x end-to-end speedup for minute-scale generation is a tangible benefit.
    *   **Maintained or Improved Fidelity:**  Quantitative results show MoC matching or exceeding the performance of dense attention baselines on VBench metrics. Qualitative results (while subjective) also support this claim.
    *   **Learned Routing:**  The model *learns* which parts of the context are relevant, rather than relying on fixed sparsity patterns or heuristics.
    *   **Comprehensive ablations:** They support the design choices such as the importance of forced links in the MoC module to ensure stable training and performance enhancement.
    *   **Generalizability:** MoC can work with DiT architecture. By integrating with the DiT backbone it shows a broad range of integration and applications in other settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While efficient, the MoC module adds complexity to the DiT architecture. The content-aligned chunking, routing mechanism, and causal masking need to be carefully implemented.
    *   **Generalization Claim needs to be further tested**: The MoC architecture works specifically well with the MMDiT implementation on video, and its application to other fields are not clear.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments**: Experiments are limited to the video generation setting, specifically fine-tuning LCT. The paper mentions potential applications to world models but does not provide any results on that.

*   **Potential Influence:** MoC has the potential to influence future research in long-context video generation, particularly in the development of efficient and scalable attention mechanisms. The idea of learned sparse routing could be applied to other sequence modeling tasks beyond video.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of video generation by offering a practical and effective solution to the long-context memory problem. While sparse attention techniques exist, the learnable routing in MoC is a novel approach that offers tangible benefits in efficiency and fidelity. The method is reasonably well-validated, with quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating its effectiveness. However, there is still the question of implementation complexity, broader applicability and overall integration simplicity.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### OnGoal: Tracking and Visualizing Conversational Goals in Multi-Turn Dialogue with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21061v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Coscia, Shunan Guo, Eunyee Koh, Alex Endert
- **Abstract**: As multi-turn dialogues with large language models (LLMs) grow longer and more complex, how can users better evaluate and review progress on their conversational goals? We present OnGoal, an LLM chat interface that helps users better manage goal progress. OnGoal provides real-time feedback on goal alignment through LLM-assisted evaluation, explanations for evaluation results with examples, and overviews of goal progression over time, enabling users to navigate complex dialogues more effectively. Through a study with 20 participants on a writing task, we evaluate OnGoal against a baseline chat interface without goal tracking. Using OnGoal, participants spent less time and effort to achieve their goals while exploring new prompting strategies to overcome miscommunication, suggesting tracking and visualizing goals can enhance engagement and resilience in LLM dialogues. Our findings inspired design implications for future LLM chat interfaces that improve goal communication, reduce cognitive load, enhance interactivity, and enable feedback to improve LLM performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OnGoal: Tracking and Visualizing Conversational Goals in Multi-Turn Dialogue with Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OnGoal, a chat interface designed to improve the evaluation and review of conversational goals in multi-turn dialogues with large language models (LLMs). OnGoal provides real-time feedback on goal alignment through LLM-assisted evaluation, explanations, and visualizations of goal progression. A user study with 20 participants performing writing tasks compares OnGoal to a baseline chat interface. The results suggest OnGoal reduces the time and effort required to achieve goals, encourages the exploration of new prompting strategies, and enhances engagement and resilience in LLM dialogues. The paper concludes with design implications for future LLM chat interfaces focused on goal communication, cognitive load reduction, enhanced interactivity, and improved feedback mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles the real challenge of managing conversational goals in complex, multi-turn LLM interactions. As these dialogues become more common, this problem will only grow in importance.
*   **Novel Interface Design:** OnGoal offers a novel and comprehensive interface design that integrates LLM-assisted goal tracking, visualization, and explanations directly within the chat environment.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper employs a well-designed user study with a clear methodology, a between-subjects design, and a quantifiable way of assessing a success metric, with NASA-TLX to assess the mental demand. The collection of data from user logs and video recordings allows to gain more insights on the interaction to give a more robust argumentation.
*   **Practical Design Implications:** The authors derive practical design implications for future LLM interfaces, offering tangible suggestions for improving goal communication, reducing cognitive load, enhancing interactivity, and providing effective feedback.
*   **Clear Communication:** The paper is well-written and clearly communicates the design, implementation, evaluation, and findings of OnGoal.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Task Specificity:** The evaluation focuses primarily on writing tasks. While these are representative of many LLM interactions, the generalizability of the findings to other domains (e.g., data analysis, code generation, or problem-solving) could be questioned. The writing tasks themselves used set goals for the LLM interactions and do not allow for real-world interactions and more natural settings.
*   **Limited Novelty in Core Techniques:** While the overall system is novel, the individual techniques used within OnGoal (e.g., LLM-based goal inference, text highlighting) are not entirely new. The novelty lies primarily in their integration and application to the problem of conversational goal management.
*   **Potential Over-Reliance on LLMs:** The system relies heavily on LLMs for goal inference and evaluation. While this allows for flexible goal representation, it also raises concerns about potential biases and inaccuracies in LLM-generated feedback. The paper reports issues with LLM evaluations, but doesn't solve them, only reporting the users felt more confused when faced with the LLM disagreement. This creates doubts on the evaluation aspect of OnGoal.
*   **Limited Evaluation of Long-Term Impact:** The user study is relatively short, and it does not assess the long-term impact of OnGoal on user behavior, performance, and satisfaction. Whether users will continue to find the system useful after prolonged use remains an open question.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by:

*   **Highlighting the importance of managing conversational goals in LLM dialogues.**
*   **Providing a concrete example of how an interface can be designed to address this challenge.**
*   **Generating valuable insights into user behavior and preferences when interacting with LLMs.**

OnGoal demonstrates the potential of combining LLMs with visualization techniques to enhance the usability and effectiveness of conversational interfaces. The design implications presented in the paper can inform the development of future systems that empower users to better manage their goals and collaborate effectively with LLMs. However, future studies need to address the weaknesses to create more strong arguments.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7** to the paper.

*   The paper addresses a relevant problem, provides a novel solution, presents rigorous evidence, offers design recommendations, and provides a relevant evaluation method.
*   However, the task-specific nature of the evaluation, the limited novelty in the core techniques, and the reliance on LLMs for goal inference and evaluation, and the limited long-term evaluation suggest it does not achieve the highest levels of novelty and impact.

In all, it is a solid contribution to the field of HCI and LLM interaction that can inspire future research and development.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### OneReward: Unified Mask-Guided Image Generation via Multi-Task Human Preference Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21066v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Gong, Xionghui Wang, Jie Wu, Shiyin Wang, Yitong Wang, Xinglong Wu
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we introduce OneReward, a unified reinforcement learning framework that enhances the model's generative capabilities across multiple tasks under different evaluation criteria using only \textit{One Reward} model. By employing a single vision-language model (VLM) as the generative reward model, which can distinguish the winner and loser for a given task and a given evaluation criterion, it can be effectively applied to multi-task generation models, particularly in contexts with varied data and diverse task objectives. We utilize OneReward for mask-guided image generation, which can be further divided into several sub-tasks such as image fill, image extend, object removal, and text rendering, involving a binary mask as the edit area. Although these domain-specific tasks share same conditioning paradigm, they differ significantly in underlying data distributions and evaluation metrics. Existing methods often rely on task-specific supervised fine-tuning (SFT), which limits generalization and training efficiency. Building on OneReward, we develop Seedream 3.0 Fill, a mask-guided generation model trained via multi-task reinforcement learning directly on a pre-trained base model, eliminating the need for task-specific SFT. Experimental results demonstrate that our unified edit model consistently outperforms both commercial and open-source competitors, such as Ideogram, Adobe Photoshop, and FLUX Fill [Pro], across multiple evaluation dimensions. Code and model are available at: https://one-reward.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "OneReward" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "OneReward," a unified reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve image generation quality across multiple tasks (image fill, extension, object removal, and text rendering) with diverse evaluation criteria.  The core idea is to use a single vision-language model (VLM) as a generative reward model, trained to distinguish between preferred and less-preferred generated images based on task type and evaluation metric (e.g., aesthetics, structure, consistency). By incorporating task category and evaluation metric information into the VLM's queries, it can effectively guide a multi-task generation model, Seedream 3.0 Fill. This approach avoids the need for task-specific supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically required by other methods.  The paper demonstrates through experiments that Seedream 3.0 Fill, trained with OneReward, outperforms several commercial and open-source competitors in image editing tasks. It also presents a dynamic RL variant using an EMA model that updates the reference, achieving enhanced performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *integration of a single VLM as a unified reward model for multi-task, multi-criteria image generation via RL*. Previous RLHF approaches either relied on multiple reward models, struggled to handle conflicting criteria within a single task, or relied on policy estimation instead of reward maximization. Using a single VLM conditioned on both task and criteria information is a significant simplification and offers better generalizability. The dynamic EMA model for a stable and adaptive reward generation is a good addition.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to simplify and improve the training of versatile image editing models. By eliminating the need for task-specific SFT, OneReward offers a more efficient and scalable approach. The experimental results demonstrate a tangible improvement in generation quality compared to existing methods, suggesting the practical value of the framework.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Framework:** The core strength is the "OneReward" framework itself, which presents a clear and compelling approach to multi-task learning in image generation.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides robust experimental validation, comparing their method against several strong baselines, and across different evaluation dimensions. The human evaluation is important.
    *   **Practical Impact:**  The demonstration of outperformance over commercial APIs like Adobe Photoshop and open-source alternatives highlights the potential practical impact.
    *   **Code Release:** Releasing the OneReward method on a open source model such as Flux fill is a very good effort.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **VLM Reliance:** The framework's performance hinges on the capabilities of the chosen VLM. While the paper uses a powerful VLM, the effectiveness may be limited by the VLM's biases and limitations. This dependence is not thoroughly discussed.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the paper compares against existing methods, a more thorough ablation study focusing on the different components of the OneReward framework itself (e.g., the query design, the RL objective) would further strengthen the analysis. More analysis of VLM's reward generation can improve the understanding.
    *   **Scalability and Computational Cost:** The paper mentions efficiency gains by avoiding SFT, but it doesn't discuss the computational cost of training the OneReward model with a VLM. More details on training time and resource requirements would be valuable.
    *   **Style Consistency limitations:** There is a mention of a relative weakness in Style Consistency for Seedream 3.0. A more in-depth analysis of this weakness and possible solutions would be valuable.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by providing a simpler and more effective RL framework for multi-task image generation. It could also inspire new approaches to reward modeling in other domains.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field, demonstrating a novel and effective approach to multi-task image generation using RL. The OneReward framework offers a compelling alternative to existing methods and is well-supported by experimental results. While there are weaknesses in terms of ablation studies and in-depth analysis of the chosen VLM's limitations, the overall strength of the framework and the significant performance gains justify a relatively high score. The dynamic RL variant also add credits. The code and model release also strengthen the impact of the work.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### First-Place Solution to NeurIPS 2024 Invisible Watermark Removal Challenge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21072v1)
- **Authors**: Fahad Shamshad, Tameem Bakr, Yahia Shaaban, Noor Hussein, Karthik Nandakumar, Nils Lukas
- **Abstract**: Content watermarking is an important tool for the authentication and copyright protection of digital media. However, it is unclear whether existing watermarks are robust against adversarial attacks. We present the winning solution to the NeurIPS 2024 Erasing the Invisible challenge, which stress-tests watermark robustness under varying degrees of adversary knowledge. The challenge consisted of two tracks: a black-box and beige-box track, depending on whether the adversary knows which watermarking method was used by the provider. For the beige-box track, we leverage an adaptive VAE-based evasion attack, with a test-time optimization and color-contrast restoration in CIELAB space to preserve the image's quality. For the black-box track, we first cluster images based on their artifacts in the spatial or frequency-domain. Then, we apply image-to-image diffusion models with controlled noise injection and semantic priors from ChatGPT-generated captions to each cluster with optimized parameter settings. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that our method successfully achieves near-perfect watermark removal (95.7%) with negligible impact on the residual image's quality. We hope that our attacks inspire the development of more robust image watermarking methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents the winning solution to the NeurIPS 2024 "Erasing the Invisible" challenge, which focused on removing invisible watermarks from images. The authors address the challenge's two tracks: a "beige-box" scenario (where the watermarking method is known) and a "black-box" scenario (where the watermarking method is unknown). For the beige-box track, they utilize an adaptive VAE-based evasion attack with test-time optimization and color contrast restoration. For the black-box track, they cluster images based on spatial/frequency domain artifacts and apply image-to-image diffusion models with controlled noise injection and semantic priors derived from ChatGPT-generated captions. The authors achieve near-perfect watermark removal while preserving image quality, outperforming competitors in both tracks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combined application and tailoring of existing techniques to specifically address the watermarking removal problem. While VAEs, diffusion models, and clustering have been used in other contexts, the authors' adaptive approach, particularly the integration of ChatGPT for semantic guidance in the black-box setting, is innovative. The test-time optimization and frequency-aware color restoration in the beige-box scenario also represent a novel combination. The discovery and exploitation of a vulnerability in the TreeRing watermarking method (spatial translation) is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it demonstrates the vulnerability of current watermarking techniques to adaptive attacks. This is particularly relevant given the increasing importance of watermarking in the age of AI-generated content. The winning solution highlights the need for more robust watermarking methods that are resilient to the types of attacks presented in the paper. The clustering strategy for the black-box attack provides a valuable framework for approaching unknown watermarking schemes. By making their attack strategy public, the authors contribute to the development of more effective defenses. The analysis of various diffusion models with differing settings offers insights regarding model performance for watermarking removal.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a comprehensive and well-documented approach to the watermark removal problem.
    *   The authors provide a clear description of their methodology and implementation details.
    *   The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach, as evidenced by their winning performance in the NeurIPS challenge.
    *   The paper includes a thorough analysis of different techniques and their trade-offs.
    *   The authors openly acknowledge limitations and present exploratory, though ultimately ineffective, approaches, showcasing thoroughness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies heavily on existing techniques; although the application and adaptation are novel, the individual components are well-established.
    *   The reliance on ChatGPT for semantic priors introduces a dependency on a potentially volatile or evolving API.
    *   The approach may be computationally expensive, particularly the test-time optimization and diffusion-based refinement stages. Although not a core evaluation metric, it could limit its wider applicability.
    *   Manual clustering is not scalable, further automation will be required in this step.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field of watermarking by highlighting the vulnerabilities of existing methods and inspiring the development of more robust defense mechanisms. The authors' approach provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating the resilience of future watermarking schemes. The combination of generative models, clustering techniques, and semantic guidance is a promising direction for future research in this area.

**Justification:**

While the paper doesn't introduce entirely new fundamental techniques, it's the clever combination and application of existing methods tailored to the specific problem that make it valuable. The analysis and exploitation of vulnerabilities in popular watermarking schemes are critical for advancing the field. The paper serves as a strong reminder of the arms race between watermarking and watermark removal, encouraging researchers to consider more sophisticated and adaptive attack models. A score of 7 reflects the valuable engineering contribution and practical impact while acknowledging the limited theoretical novelty.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
